F.10    Proof of Proposition 25

Proof. First, we deal with the case when the coalition weighting function µ(·) is finite such that µ(S) ∈ R+ for all

                                                                           2

convex function:

             X ∈ {0, 1}2 ×d` indexed with XS,T = 1[(T ⊆ S) ∨ (T = Ø)], where S ⊆ [d] and T ∈ S` .

can also be expressed as XS = p(S), where p(S) ∈ Rd` with p(S)[T ] = 1[(T ⊆ S) ∨ (T = Ø)].
Then we define the weight matrix:
           √              d  d                                                  √      p
             W ∈ {0, 1}2 ×2 is a diagonal matrix with each entry on the diagonal WS,S = µ(S),
where S ⊆ [d]. The function values of v(·) on each subset can be written into a vector:
                                           d
                                  Y ∈ R2 indexed with YS = v(S) where S ⊆ [d].

                                   √                      2
                                                                                        2
                                       W(Y − XE(v, `))        = kYw − Xw E(v, `)k2 ,                            (50)
                                                          2
              √                   √
where Yw =        WY and Xw =         WX. The Hessian matrix can be expressed as 2Xw T Xw .
We now prove that the Hessian matrix is positive definite. Let b be any vector in Rd` .
                                             
                                                               2
                                                                      √          2
                             bT 2Xw T Xw b = 2 kXw bk2 = 2               WXb ≥ 0.
                                                                                    2
                                                 √                       √
We get that bT Xw T Xw b = 0 if and only if WXb = 0. Since W is a diagonal matrix with each entry is a
                                               2
positive number, we get
                            √
                               WXb = 0 ⇔ Xb = 0 ⇔ p(S)b = 0, ∀ S ⊆ [d].
                                           2

We prove that this also implies that bL = 0 for all L ∈ S` by using induction on the size of T .

       1. If we plug in S = Ø, we get bØ = 0.
       2. Assume bL = 0 for all L ⊆ [d] with |L| ≤ k.
       3. For all subsets L with size k + 1 ≤ `, we have
                                              X                    X
                                                   bT = bL +                  bT = 0.
                                               T ⊆L             T ⊆L,|T |≤k

          Therefore, we have bL = 0.

We obtain that bT Xw T Xw b = 0 if and only if b = 0. Therefore, the Hessian matrix is positive definite and Eqn.(49)
is strictly convex.

F.12   Proof of Proposition 27

Proof. First, by Proposition 26, Eqn.(10) has a unique minimizer. Next, we prove that ES (v, d) = a(v, S) is the only
minimzer. Specifically, Grabisch et al. [18] has shown that ES (v, d) = a(v, S) satisfies

This implies that Eqn.(10) is zero. However, since Eqn.(10) is always non-negative, ES (v, d) = a(v, S) is the only
minimizer.

Also, the following claim states that the operator Dit is positive for all t, i ∈ {0, 1, 2, ..., d} with 0 ≤ t + i ≤ d.

By Proposition 13, the Faith-Interaction index satisfies interaction linearity axiom. In addition, by Proposition 14,
since the weighting µ(S) only depends on |S|, the Faith-Interaction index also satisfies the interaction symmetry
axiom. Consequently, we only need to prove that the Faith-Interaction index satisfies the interaction dummy axiom.
Next, we introduce the basis function.
Definition 38. For any subset R ⊆ [d] with |R| = r, the basis function with respect to R is defined below:
                                                      
                                                        1, if S ⊇ R.
                                            vR (S) =
                                                        0, otherwise.

This is known as unanimity game in game theory community. We note that only elements inside R actually contribute
to the function value. That is, elements belong to [d]\R are dummy elements. Formally, we have v(S ∪ i) = v(S) for
any i ∈ [d]\R and S ⊆ [d]\{i}.
An important property of the basis functions is that any function v : {0, 1}d → R can be expressed as a linear
combination of the 2d basis functions. Then by the interaction linearity axiom, the minimizer of Eqn.(9) with respect
to v(·) can be represented as the same linear combination of minimizer of these basis functions. In the following
lemma, we show that if the minimizers of Eqn.(9) with respect to these 2d basis functions satisfy the interaction
dummy axiom, then all functions satisfy dummy axiom. Therefore, it is sufficient to prove that these minimizers
satisfy the interaction dummy axiom for these 2d basis functions.
Lemma 39. Let E(v, `) be a Faith-Interaction indice with respect to a proper weighting function µ : 2d 7→ R+ ∪{∞}.
If ES (vR , `) = 0 for all ` ∈ [d] and for all S ∈ S` , R ⊆ [d] with S ∩ ([d]\R) 6= Ø, then the Faith-Interaction indices
with respect to the weighting function µ(·) satisfy interaction dummy axiom.

Proof. By Lemma 3 in Shapley [46], any function v : {0, 1}d → R can be expressed as a linear combination of these
2d basis functions, such that
                                   X                        X
                              v=        cR vR with cR =        (−1)|R|−|T | v(T ).
                                       R⊆[d]                       T ⊆R

cR here is the Möbius coefficient. By Proposition 13, the Faith-Interaction indice E satisfies the interaction linearity
axiom, which implies that E(v, `) can be expressed as the following form:
                                                        X
                                            E(v, `) =        cR E(vR , `).
                                                           R⊆[d]

Suppose that we have some dummy feature i ∈ [d] such that v(T ∪ i) = v(T ) for any T ⊆ [d]\{i}, then for any
R ⊇ {i}, we have
                        X                        X
                   cR =     (−1)|R|−|T | v(T ) =       (−1)|R|−|T | (v(T ) − v(T ∪ i)) = 0.
                            T ⊆R                       T ⊆R\{i}

Therefore, the function v is the linear combinations of vR (·) for some R not containing i. However, for these subsets,
by the definition of the basis function, we have vR (S) = vR (S ∪ i) for any S ⊆ [d]\{i}. Since we have ES (vR , `) = 0
for all S ∈ S` with S ∩ ([d]\R) 6= Ø, we get ET (vR , `) = 0 for any T containing the dummy feature i. Consequently,
we have                                    X                       X
                               ET (v, `) =     cR ET (vR , `) =           cR ET (vR , `) = 0.
                                           R⊆[d]                   R⊆[d]\{i}

We note that the above proof holds for any ` ∈ [d]. Therefore, the Faith-Interaction indices with respect to µ(·) satisfy
interaction dummy axiom.

Now we come back to the proof of Theorem 16. By Lemma 39, we only need to prove that for any R ⊆ [d], the
minimizer of Eqn.(9) with respect to the basis function vR , E(vR , `), satisfies ES (vR , `) = 0 if S containing any
dummy element in [d]\R.

Now we utilize the symmetric structure in the basis function vR (·) and weighting function µ(·). In the basis functions,
there are only two kinds of input elements, which are elements in R and not in R. Therefore, for ith order interactions
terms ET (vR , `) where |T | = i, there are at most i + 1 distinct values. Each value corresponds to the set with j
elements in R for j = 0, 1, ...i. That is, by the interaction symmetry axiom, there are only i + 1 different importance
value for ith order interactions terms (since if |T1 | = |T2 | and |T1 ∩ R| = |T2 ∩ R| then T1 and T2 are symmetric and
ET1 (vR , `) = ET2 (vR , `)), so there are 1 + 2 + ... + (` + 1) = (`+2)(`+1)

Note that we have ρ ≤ |A ∩ R| = i − j and σ ≤ |A\R| = j. Since every S can map to some constraints p, q, ρ, σ, by
summation over all possible p, q, ρ, σ, we can get the partial derivative of the objective with respect to bi,j .
Also, if there are not enough elements to be selected, i.e. |R\A| < p0 − q0 − ρ0 , p0|R\A|
                                                                                                 
                                                                                          −q0 −ρ0 = 0, so the number of
ways for selection is zero.

Lemma 45. Assume we have a system of υ linear equations with υ unknowns, Av = C, where A ∈ Rυ×υ is the
coefficient matrix, v ∈ Rυ is a vector of unknowns, and C ∈ Rυ is a vector of real numbers. Define P ⊆ {1, 2, ..., υ}
as a set of indexes, and denote AP ∈ Rυ×|P | as a submatrix of A by only taking columns in A whose indexes are in
P . If Av = C has a unique solution v and rank([AP , C]) = |P |, then the solution vi = 0 if i ∈
                                                                                               / P.

Proof. Since Av = C has a unique solution, by Rouché–Capelli theorem, we have rank(A) = rank([A, C]) = υ,
which equals to the number of columns in A. Hence, the columns of A are linear independent. The column spaces of
AP consists of |P | columns from A, so we have rank(AP ) = |P |.
Let vP ∈ R|P | consist of values in x whose indexes are in P . By Rouché–Capelli theorem, rank(AP ) =
rank([AP , C]) = |P | implies the system of linear equations, AP vP = C, has a unique solution vP . Now we
construct the solution of Av = C by vP : let
                                      vP j if i ∈ P, where i the j th element of P
                                    
                               vi =
                                      0 , otherwise,
where vP j denote the j th element of vP . We can easily verify that v is the unique solution of Av = C.


Lemma 45 tells us that if we aim to prove that some unknown variables are zero in a system of linear equations, we
can alternatively prove that the rank of a simplified augmented matrix [AP , C] equals |P |.
Then, if the interaction dummy axiom holds, the terms bi,j with i ≥ j > 0 should be zero since these interaction
terms contain at least one dummy element ( that is outside R). Therefore, by Lemma 45, we now consider columns
corresponding to b0,0 , b1,0 , ..., b`,0 . These columns correspond to interaction terms that only contain elements in R.
We simplify the matrix M in the following way:

Lemma 53. Given the results in Lemma 51 and following Definition 46, then
                                            A = {<00,0 (Q), <11,0 (Q), ..., <``,0 (Q)}
forms the basis of row space of matrix Q and therefore implies that rank(Q) = ` + 1.

Proof. We prove a stronger version of this lemma: <ki,j (Q) lies in the span of A for all 0 ≤ i ≤ `, 0 ≤ k ≤ i, and
0 ≤ j ≤ i − k. This result immediately implies that the row Q{i,j} = <0i,j in matrix Q also lies in the span of A.
We prove it by induction on two indices, which are i and k.

     1. When i = 0, k and j can only be zero. We have <00,0 (Q) ∈ span(A).

     2. When i = i0 , suppose that <ki,j (Q) ∈ span(A) holds for 0 ≤ i ≤ i0 , 0 ≤ k ≤ i and 0 ≤ j ≤ i − k.

     3. When i = i0 + 1, we prove that <ki0 +1,j (Q) lies in the span of A for all 0 ≤ k ≤ i0 + 1, and 0 ≤ j ≤ i − k
        by using another induction on k. In this induction process, k is taking value from i0 + 1 to 0.
                                                  0
         (a) When k = i0 + 1, we know <ii0 +1,i0 +1 ∈ span(A) by plugging s = 0 and t = i0 + 1 in Lemma 51 .
                          0
         (b) Suppose <ki0 +1,j (Q) ∈ span(A) for k = k 0 + 1, .., i0 + 1 and 0 ≤ j ≤ i0 + 1 − k for some 0 ≤ k 0 ≤ i0
         (c) When k = k 0 , by Lemma 51, we know
                                   0                                                    0                0
                               <ki0 +1,i0 −k0 (Q) ∈ span(<0i0 −k0 ,i0 −k0 (Q), ..., <ki0 ,i0 −k0 (Q), <ki0 +1,i

        0 ≤ k ≤ i0 + 1 and 0 ≤ j ≤ i0 + 1 − k. Consequently, we also finish the induction step on i (when i = i0 + 1).


                                                                  54
                                                                                                       A PREPRINT


Finally, we can deduce that <ki,j (Q) lies in the span of A for any 0 ≤ i ≤ `, 0 ≤ j ≤ i and 0 ≤ k ≤ i − j. Then we
know every row Q{i,j} = <0i,j (Q) in matrix Q also lies in the span of A. It immediately implies rank(Q) = ` + 1.



By Lemma 53, the rank of the matrix Q = [M0 , Y] equals to ` + 1. This in turn implies that bi,j = 0 for all
i ≥ j > 0 by Lemma 45. Therefore, the corresponding Faith-Interaction indices satisfy the interaction dummy axiom
when d ≥ ` + r and r > `.

(3) r + ` > d ≥ ` + r and r > `: Now, we generalize the results in the second case to the last case. We recall
that bi,j denotes the interaction indices with j elements in R and j elements in [d]\R (Definition 41). However, when
r + ` > d, there are some bi,j that do not exist since there are not enough elements outside R. For example, b`,` does
not exist since there are only d − r < ` elements outside R.
In this case, we can still compute the matrix of linear equations, Q0 = [M0 , Y], but some rows do not exist. Particu-
larly, all rows corresponding to bi,j with d − r < j ≤ i do not exist. Nevertheless, removing rows do not increases
the rank of the matrix Q. Therefore, by Lemma 53, the rank of rank(Q0 ) ≤ rank(Q) = ` + 1. Also, we note that the
columns in the coefficient matrix M are linearly independent (since it has a unique solution by Proposition 26). This
implies that the columns in the reduced coefficient matrix M0 are also linearly independent (since M0 is a submatrix
of M). Therefore, the rank of the reduced coefficient matrix M0 equals to the number of columns in M0 , which is
` + 1.
Overall, we have ` + 1 ≥ rank(Q0 ) ≥ rank(M0 ) = ` + 1. That is, rank(Q0 ) = ` + 1, which in turn implies that
bi,j = 0 for all i ≥ j > 0 by Lemma 45. Therefore, the corresponding Faith-Interaction indices satisfy the interaction
dummy axiom when r + ` > d ≥ ` + r and r > `.
Therefore, by summarizing (1)-(3), we conclude that the interaction dummy axiom holds for all basis function vR .
This result can be generalized to any function v(·) by applying Lemma 39.
In conclusion, the Faith-Interaction indices with respect to the weighting function defined in Eqn.(12) satisfy the
interaction linearity, symmetry and dummy axioms for all set functions v(·) : 2d 7→ R and all maximum interaction
order 1 ≤ ` ≤ d.

G.2.2   Proof of Necessary Condition of Theorem 16
Now we prove the necessary condition of Theorem 16. That is, Faith-Interaction indices E with a finite weighting
function satisfy interaction linearity, symmetry, and dummy axioms only if the weighting function µ has the form in
Eqn.(12).
From Proposition 14, the Faithful-Interaction indices satisfy interaction symmetry axiom if and only if µ(S) only
depends on the size of the input set |S|. Therefore, the weighting function must be symmetric. The following lemma
show that the weighting function must be in the form in Eqn.(12) if the corresponding Faith-Interaction indices satisfy
interaction dummy axiom.
Lemma 54. Faith-Interaction indices E with a finite and permutation-invariant weighting function satisfy the inter-

G.4     Proof of Theorem 19

In this section, we present proof for the sufficient condition, the closed-form expressions of Faithful Shapley index,
and the necessary condition of Theorem 19. For the sufficient condition, we provide two proof. The first one is in
Section G.4.1 and is simpler. The second one is in Section G.4.2 and is similar to the proof of Theorem 16. Next,
we derive the closed-form expression of Faithful Shapley Interaction indices in Section G.4.3. Finally, we provide the
proof of the necessary condition in Section G.4.4.

G.4.1    Proof of Sufficient Condition of Theorem 19
In the following, we prove that under the weighting function defined in Eqn. (15), the minimizers of Eqn.(10) satisfy
interaction linearity, symmetry, efficiency, and dummy axioms.
Interaction linearity, symmetry, efficiency axiom: The minimizers of Eqn.(10) satisfy interaction linearity, symme-
try, and efficiency axioms by Proposition 13, 14 and 18.
Interaction dummy axiom: Below we prove that the optimal solution satisfies the interaction dummy axiom. The
constrained optimization problem can be written as follows:

the following lemma:
Lemma 57. Assume that ith feature of the set function v(·) is a dummy feature such that v(S) = v(S ∪ i) for all

G.4.2   Alternative proof of Sufficient Condition of Theorem 19
In the following, we present an alternative proof of sufficient condition of that under the weighting function defined in
Eqn. (15), the minimizers of Theorem 19. This proof is similar to Theorem 16.
Interaction linearity, symmetry, efficiency axiom: The minimizers of Eqn.(10) satisfy interaction linearity, symme-
try, and efficiency axioms by Proposition 13, 14 and 18.
Interaction dummy axiom: Below we prove that the optimal solution satisfies the interaction dummy axiom. The
constrained optimization problem can be written as follows:

know that every row Q{i,j} = <0i,j (Q) in matrix Q also lies in the span of A, which in turn implies rank(Q) = ` + 1.

(3) r + ` > d ≥ ` + r and r > `: Now, we generalize the results in the second case to the last case. We recall
that bi,j denotes the interaction indices with j elements in R and j elements in [d]\R (Definition 41). However, when
r + ` > d, there are some bi,j that do not exist since there are not enough elements outside R. For example, b`,` does
not exist since there are only d − r < ` elements outside R.
In this case, we can still compute the matrix of linear equations, Q0 = [M0 , Y], but some rows do not exist. Particu-
larly, all rows corresponding to bi,j with d − r < j ≤ i do not exist. Nevertheless, removing rows do not increases
the rank of the matrix Q. Therefore, by Lemma 67, the rank of rank(Q0 ) ≤ rank(Q) = ` + 1. Also, we note that the
columns in the coefficient matrix M are linearly independent (since it has a unique solution by Proposition 26). This
implies that the columns in the reduced coefficient matrix M0 are also linearly independent (since M0 is a submatrix
of M). Therefore, the rank of the reduced coefficient matrix M0 equals to the number of columns in M0 , which is
` + 1.
Overall, we have ` + 1 ≥ rank(Q0 ) ≥ rank(M0 ) = ` + 1. That is, rank(Q0 ) = ` + 1, which in turn implies that
bi,j = 0 for all i ≥ j > 0 by Lemma 45. Therefore, the corresponding Faith-Interaction indices satisfy the interaction
dummy axiom when r + ` > d ≥ ` + r and r > `.
Therefore, by summarizing (1)-(3), we conclude that the interaction dummy axiom holds for all basis function vR .
This result can be generalized to any function v(·) by applying Lemma 39.
In conclusion, the Faith-Interaction indices with respect to the weighting function defined in Eqn.(12) satisfy the
interaction linearity, symmetry and dummy axioms for all set functions v(·) : 2d 7→ R and all maximum interaction
order 1 ≤ ` ≤ d.

                                                                                                         A PREPRINT


G.4.3   Closed-form Solution of Faith-Shap
In this section, we solve the constrained weighted linear regression problem defined in Eqn.(10) with the weighting
function defined in Eqn.(15). We start with solving Faith-Shap indices for basis functions (Lemma 68) and then extend
the results to general set functions (Lemma 70). Lastly, we provide another expression for the highest order terms of
Faithful Shapley Interaction indices, i.e. ES (v, `) for |S| = `, in terms of discrete derivatives (Lemma 71).
First of all, we solve the closed-form solution when the set function v(·) is a basis function.

G.4.4   Proof of Necessary Condition of Theorem 19
Now we prove the necessary condition of Theorem 19. That is, Faith-Interaction indices E satisfy linearity, symmetry,
efficiency and dummy axioms only if the weighting function µ has the form in Eqn.(15).
From Proposition 14, the Faithful-Interaction indices satisfy the interaction symmetry axiom if and only if µ(S) only
depends on the size of the input set |S|. Therefore, the weighting function must be symmetric. Also, by Proposition
18, Faithful-Interaction indices satisfy the interaction efficiency axiom if and only if µ(Ø) = µ([d]) = ∞. Therefore,
the weighting function must be permutation-invariant and has infinity measure on the empty set and the full set.
Then, the following lemma show that the weighting function must be in the form of Eqn.(15) if the corresponding
Faith-Interaction indices additionally satisfy dummy axiom.
Lemma 73. Faith-Interaction indices E with a weighting function, which is permutation-invariant and has µ(Ø) =
µ([d]) = ∞, satisfy the interaction dummy axiom only if the weighting function µ has the following form:
                            d−1
            µ(S) ∝ d                    for all S ⊆ [d] with 1 ≤ |S| ≤ d − 1, and µ(Ø) = µ([d]) = ∞
                      |S|  |S| (d − |S|)

Proof. We now solve the case when the function v = vR is a basis function for some R ⊆ [d]. Recall that the
definition of basis functions is :                     
                                                         1, if S ⊇ R
                                             vR (S) =
                                                         0, otherwise.
Since the weighting function µ(·) if finite and only depends on the size of the input set, we use the definitions in
                                                        Pd
Section G.1: µ|S| = µ(S) and µ̄|S| = T ⊇S µ(T ) = i=|S| d−|S|
                                       P                               
                                                                 i−|S|
                                                                         µi for all S ⊆ [d]. Since Faithful-Interaction
indices should hold for all maximum interaction orders 1 ≤ ` ≤ d, we restrict the maximum interaction order to ` = 1.
We now use the following results from Ding et al. [9].
Proposition 74. (Ding et al. [9], Theorem 16) When the maximum interaction order ` = 1 (no interaction terms)
and the set function v = vR is a basis function for some R ⊆ [d] with |R| = r, and the weighting function µ(S) is
permutation-invariant and has µ(Ø) = µ([d]) = ∞, the minimizer of Eqn.(10), E(vR , `) ∈ Rd+1 , has the following
form:                                    

H.13    Proof of Claim 76

Proof. We prove a more general form: for any a, b ∈ N + {0}, we have
                               Z
                                                                                      a!
                    ga,b (x) = xa (1 − x)b dx = (1 − x)b+1 pa,b (x) + Qa                        ,                (110)
                                                                                k=0 (b + k + 1)

where ga,b (x), pa,b (x) are polynomials depended on a, b with ga,b (0) = 0 .
Now we prove Eqn.(110) by induction on a. For a = 0, we have

                                                     −(1 − x)b+1
                                   Z
                                                                    1
                                     (1 − x)b dx =               +     .
                                                        b+1        b+1

                                                          88
                                                                                                       A PREPRINT


Assume that Eqn.(110) holds for a = n. Then, for a, b ∈ N, using integration by parts, we have
                             Z
                                 xn+1 (1 − x)b dx
                                  −1
                                       Z
                              =           xn+1 d(1 − x)b+1
                                 b+1
                                  −1 n+1
                                                                Z
                                                            1
                              =        x   (1 − x)b+1 +            (1 − x)b+1 dxn+1
                                 b+1                      b+1
                                  −1 n+1
                                                                 Z
                                                   b+1    n+1
                              =        x   (1 − x)      +           xn (1 − x)b+1 dx.
                                 b+1                      b+1
We note that the first term is divisible by (1 − x)b+1 , while the second term can be obtained by induction hypothesis.
Therefore, we have
                                           Z
                             gn+1,b (x) = xn+1 (1 − x)b dx
                                                                n+1          n!
                                      = (1 − x)b+1 pn+1,b (x) +       Qn

                                                                    (n + 1)!
                                      = (1 − x)b+1 pn+1,b (x) + Qn+1              .
                                                                  k=0 (b + k + 1)

By plugging in a = r − ` − 1 and b = ` − 1, we have the desired results. Also, since the degree of p1 (x) =
R r−`−1
 x      (1 − x)`−i dx is at most r − i, p(x) should have degree less or equal to r − ` − 1.
